Manipulating strings

str functions to know for manipulating strings

  • str_length()
  • str_sub()
  • str_c()
  • str_to_lower()
  • str_to_upper()
  • str_to_title()
  • str_replace() not in 02_strings examples
library(tidyverse)

#spotify <- read_csv("Data/spotify.csv") 
spotify <- read_csv("https://joeroith.github.io/264_spring_2026/Data/spotify.csv")

spot_smaller <- spotify |>
  select(
    title, 
    artist, 
    album_release_date, 
    album_name, 
    subgenre, 
    playlist_name
  )

Important functions for identifying strings which match

str_view() : most useful for testing str_subset() : useful for printing matches to the console str_detect() : useful when working within a tibble

  1. Identify the input type and output type for each of these examples:
str_view(spot_smaller$subgenre, "pop")
# input = chr vector
# output = chr vector using ANSI escapes

typeof(str_view(spot_smaller$subgenre, "pop"))
# input = any R object; in this case it is the result of str_view = vector
# output = character string

class(str_view(spot_smaller$subgenre, "pop"))
# input = any R object; in this case it is the result of str_view = vector
# output = character string

str_view(spot_smaller$subgenre, "pop", match = NA)
# based on the help document, match = NA will show all elements-- regardless if it matches the pattern or not. 
# input = chr vector
# output = chr vector using ANSI escapes

str_view(spot_smaller$subgenre, "pop", html = TRUE)
# input = chr vector
# output = vector in HTML formatting/widget

str_subset(spot_smaller$subgenre, "pop")
# input = vector
# output = vector
# should both be character

str_detect(spot_smaller$subgenre, "pop")
# input = vector
# vector = logical vector

  1. Find the mean song title length for songs with “pop” in the subgenre and songs without “pop” in the subgenre.
spot_smaller
# A tibble: 10 × 6
   title             artist album_release_date album_name subgenre playlist_name
   <chr>             <chr>  <chr>              <chr>      <chr>    <chr>

 2 Run the World (G… Beyon… 2011-06-24         4          post-te… post-teen al…
 3 Formation         Beyon… 2016-04-23         Lemonade   hip pop  Feeling Acco…
 4 7/11              Beyon… 2014-11-24         BEYONCÉ [… hip pop  Feeling Acco…
 5 My Oh My (feat. … Camil… 2019-12-06         Romance    latin p… 2020 Hits & …
 6 It's Automatic    Frees… 2013-11-28         It's Auto… latin h… 80's Freesty…
 7 Poetic Justice    Kendr… 2012               good kid,… hip hop  Hip Hop Cont…
 8 A.D.H.D           Kendr… 2011-07-02         Section.80 souther… Hip-Hop 'n R…
 9 Ya Estuvo         Kid F… 1990-01-01         Hispanic … latin h… HIP-HOP: Lat…
10 Runnin (with A$A… Mike … 2018-11-16         Creed II:… gangste… RAP Gangsta  
spot_smaller |>
  mutate(sub_pop = 
           ifelse(str_detect(subgenre, "pop"), 
                  "Genre with pop", 
                  "Genre without pop")) |>
  group_by(sub_pop) |>
  summarise(mean_title_length = mean(str_length(title)))
# A tibble: 2 × 2
  sub_pop           mean_title_length
  <chr>                         <dbl>
1 Genre with pop                 13.6
2 Genre without pop              18.6

Producing a table like this would be great:

# A tibble: 2 × 2
  sub_pop mean_title_length
  <lgl>               <dbl>
1 FALSE                18.6
2 TRUE                 13.6

# A tibble: 2 × 2

1 Genre with pop                 13.6
2 Genre without pop              18.6
  1. In the bigspotify dataset, find the proportion of songs which contain “love” in the title (track_name) by playlist_genre.
bigspotify <- readr::read_csv('https://raw.githubusercontent.com/rfordatascience/tidytuesday/master/data/2020/2020-01-21/spotify_songs.csv')

bigspotify

#   playlist_name <chr>, playlist_id <chr>, playlist_genre <chr>,
#   playlist_subgenre <chr>, danceability <dbl>, energy <dbl>, key <dbl>,
#   loudness <dbl>, mode <dbl>, speechiness <dbl>, acousticness <dbl>,
#   instrumentalness <dbl>, liveness <dbl>, valence <dbl>, tempo <dbl>,
#   duration_ms <dbl>
bigspotify |>
  drop_na(track_name) |>
  mutate(track_name = str_to_lower(track_name),
         title_love = str_detect(track_name, "love")) |>
  select(track_name, playlist_genre, title_love) |>
  group_by(playlist_genre) |>
  summarise(prop_love = mean(title_love == "TRUE"))

4 r&b               0.0639
5 rap               0.0125
6 rock              0.0450

Matching patterns with regular expressions

^abc string starts with exactly “abc”
abc$ string ends with exactly “abc”
. any character (helpful as a placeholder)
[^abc] anything EXCEPT a or b or c 
# Guess the output!

str_view(spot_smaller$artist, "^K")

str_view(spot_smaller$album_release_date, "01$")

str_view(spot_smaller$title, "^.. ")

str_view(spot_smaller$artist, "[^A-Za-z ]")
  1. Given the corpus of common words in stringr::words, create regular expressions that find all words that:
  • Start with “y”.
  • End with “x”
  • Are exactly three letters long.
  • Have seven letters or more.
  • Start with a vowel.
  • End with ed, but not with eed.
  • Words where q is not followed by u. (are there any in words?)
str_view(stringr::words, "^y") 

str_view(stringr::words, "x$")

str_view(stringr::words, "^...$")

str_view(stringr::words, "^.......")

str_view(stringr::words, "^[aiueo]") 

str_view(stringr::words, "[^e]ed$")

str_view(stringr::words, "q[^u]")
# Try using str_view() or str_subset()

# For example, to find words with "tion" at any point, I could use:
str_view(words, "tion")
str_subset(words, "tion")

More useful regular expressions:

\\d any number
\\s any space, tab, etc.
\\b any boundary: space, ., etc. 
str_view(spot_smaller$album_name, "\\d")
str_view(spot_smaller$album_name, "\\s")
str_view(spot_smaller$album_name, "\\b")

Here are the regular expression special characters that require an escape character (a preceding \): ^ $ . ? * | + ( ) [ {

For any characters with special properties, use \ to “escape” its special meaning … but \ is itself a special character … so we need two \\! (e.g. \\$, \\., etc.)

str_view(spot_smaller$title, "$")
str_view(spot_smaller$title, "\\$")
  1. In bigspotify, how many track_names include a $? Be sure you print the track_names you find and make sure the dollar sign is not just in a featured artist!

  1. In bigspotify, how many track_names include a dollar amount (a $ followed by a number).
bigspotify |>
  filter(str_detect(track_name, "\\$\\d")) |>
  select(track_name, track_artist)
# A tibble: 2 × 2
  track_name                  track_artist
  <chr>                       <chr>       
1 $20 Fine                    Jimi Hendrix
2 $100 (feat. Polo Donatello) Mibbs

filter(str_detect(track_name, “\\(\\d")) or filter(str_detect(track_name, "\\\)\[0-9]”))

Repetition

? 0 or 1 times
+ 1 or more
* 0 or more
{n} exactly n times
{n,} n or more times
{,m} at most m times
{n,m} between n and m times 
str_view(spot_smaller$album_name, "[A-Z]{2,}")
str_view(spot_smaller$album_release_date, "\\d{4}-\\d{2}")

Use at least 1 repetition symbol when solving 8-10 below

  1. Modify the first regular expression above to also pick up “A.A” (in addition to “BEYONC” and “II”). That is, pick up strings where there might be a period between capital letters.
str_view(spot_smaller$album_name, "[A-Z]\\.?[A-Z]")
  1. Create some strings that satisfy these regular expressions and explain.
  • "^.*$"
  • "\\{.+\\}"
str_view("$uper", "^.*$")
str_view("", "^.*$")

Here are the regular expression special characters that require an escape character (a preceding \): ^ $ . ? * | + ( ) [ {

str_view("{5}", "\\{.+\\}")

str_view("{this one work!}", "\\{.+\\}")

str_view("this one does not", "\\{.+\\}")

str_view("hi", "\\{.+\\}")

double backslash… ESCAPE –> next character is the curly bracket. That means we are escaping the curly bracket. Why are we escaping it? Because the curly bracket is also a function to something else! We use curly bracket for repetition! So here, we are asking it to find an actual curly bracket Then the next thing is dot. Meaning, any THING! ANY CHARACTER! Then, the plus sign –> meaning “1 or more”.

  1. Create regular expressions to find all stringr::words that:
  • Start with three consonants.
  • Have two or more vowel-consonant pairs in a row.
str_view(stringr::words, "^[^aeiuo]{3}")

# has to be vowel then consonant pair
str_view(stringr::words, "([aiueo][^aiueo]|[aiueo][^aiueo]){2,}")

# either vowel-consonant or consonant-vowel pair.
str_view(stringr::words, "([aiueo][^aiueo]|[^aiueo][aiueo]){2,}")

Useful functions for handling patterns

str_extract() : extract a string that matches a pattern str_count() : count how many times a pattern occurs within a string

str_extract(spot_smaller$album_release_date, "\\d{4}-\\d{2}")
 [1] "2016-01" "2011-06" "2016-04" "2014-11" "2019-12" "2013-11" NA       
 [8] "2011-07" "1990-01" "2018-11"
spot_smaller |>
  select(album_release_date) |>
  mutate(year_month = str_extract(album_release_date, "\\d{4}-\\d{2}"))
# A tibble: 10 × 2
   album_release_date year_month
   <chr>              <chr>     
 1 2016-01-01         2016-01   
 2 2011-06-24         2011-06   
 3 2016-04-23         2016-04   
 4 2014-11-24         2014-11   
 5 2019-12-06         2019-12   
 6 2013-11-28         2013-11   
 7 2012               <NA>      
 8 2011-07-02         2011-07   
 9 1990-01-01         1990-01   
10 2018-11-16         2018-11   
spot_smaller |>
  select(artist) |>
  mutate(n_vowels = str_count(artist, "[aeiou]"))
# A tibble: 10 × 2
   artist            n_vowels
   <chr>                <int>
 1 Alok                     1
 2 Beyoncé                  2

 4 Beyoncé                  2
 5 Camila Cabello           6
 6 Freestyle                3
 7 Kendrick Lamar           4

 9 Kid Frost                2

  1. In the spot_smaller dataset, extract the first word from every title. Show how you would print out these words as a vector and how you would create a new column on the spot_smaller tibble. That is, produce this:
# [1] "Hear"      "Run"       "Formation" "7/11"      "My"        "It's"     
# [7] "Poetic"    "A.D.H.D"   "Ya"        "Runnin"

Then this:

# A tibble: 10 × 2
#   title                                             first_word
#   <chr>                                             <chr>     
# 1 Hear Me Now                                       Hear      
# 2 Run the World (Girls)                             Run       
# 3 Formation                                         Formation 
# 4 7/11                                              7/11      
# 5 My Oh My (feat. DaBaby)                           My        
# 6 It's Automatic                                    It's      
# 7 Poetic Justice                                    Poetic    
# 8 A.D.H.D                                           A.D.H.D   
# 9 Ya Estuvo                                         Ya        
#10 Runnin (with A$AP Rocky, A$AP Ferg & Nicki Minaj) Runnin    
str_extract(spot_smaller$title, "(^\\b[A-Z][a-z]+\\b|^\\b[A-Z].+\\b|^\\b\\d.+\\b)")
 [1] "Hear"      "Run"       "Formation" "7/11"      "My"        "It"       
 [7] "Poetic"    "A.D.H.D"   "Ya"        "Runnin"   
spot_smaller |>
  select(title) |>
  mutate(first_word = str_extract(title, "(^\\b[A-Z][a-z]+\\b|^\\b[A-Z].+\\b|^\\b\\d.+\\b)"))
# A tibble: 10 × 2
   title                                             first_word
   <chr>                                             <chr>     
 1 Hear Me Now                                       Hear      
 2 Run the World (Girls)                             Run       
 3 Formation                                         Formation 
 4 7/11                                              7/11      
 5 My Oh My (feat. DaBaby)                           My        
 6 It's Automatic                                    It        
 7 Poetic Justice                                    Poetic    
 8 A.D.H.D                                           A.D.H.D   
 9 Ya Estuvo                                         Ya

  select(playlist_name) |>
  filter(str_detect(playlist_name, "(\\d+['s]|\\d+'s)")) |>
  mutate(decades = str_extract(playlist_name, "(\\d+'s|\\d+s|\\d+s-\\d+s)")) |>
  print(n = Inf)

bigspotify |>
  select(playlist_name) |>
  filter(str_detect(playlist_name, "\\d+'s")) |>
  mutate(decades = str_extract(playlist_name, "\\d+'s"),
         decades = fct_recode(decades, "80's" = "808's",
                              "00's" = "2000's")) |>
  print(n = Inf)

#3: I don't actually understand what the sentence wants so...
bigspotify |>
  select(playlist_name) |>
  filter(str_detect(playlist_name, "\\d+'s")) |>
  mutate(decades = str_extract(playlist_name, "\\d+'s"),
         decades = fct_recode(decades, "80's" = "808's",
                              "00's" = "2000's")) |>
  count(decades, playlist_name) |>
  count(decades)
# A tibble: 4 × 2
  decades     n
  <fct>   <int>
1 00's        1
2 70's        1
3 80's        6
4 90's        5
#4 & #5
bigspotify |>
  select(playlist_name) |>
  filter(str_detect(playlist_name, "(\\d+'s|\\d+s)")) |>
  mutate(decades = str_extract(playlist_name, "(\\d+'s|\\d+s)"),
         decades = fct_recode(decades, "80's" = "808's",
                              "00's" = "2000's",
                              "50's" = "1950s",
                              "10's" = "2010s"),
         decades = fct_collapse(decades, 
                                "80's" = c("80's", "80s"),
                                "90's" = c("90's", "90s"),
                                "70's" = c("70's", "70s")
                                )) |>
  count(decades, playlist_name) |>
  count(decades)
# A tibble: 6 × 2
  decades     n
  <fct>   <int>
1 50's        1
2 00's        1
3 10's        2
4 70's        4
5 80's       10
6 90's       13

Grouping and backreferences

# find all fruits with repeated pair of letters.  
fruit = stringr::fruit
fruit
str_view(fruit, "(..)\\1", match = TRUE)

# why does the code below add "pepper" and even "nectarine"?
str_view(fruit, "(..)(.*)\\1", match = TRUE)
# because of the (.*)! because that is saying that the (..) is followed by ANY CHARACTER 0 or more time... before repeating (..)

str_view(fruit, “(..)\1”, match = TRUE) (..) any 2 characters \1 repeating what we have established above. that’s why we are getting “anan” “coco” “cucu” “juju” etc.

str_view(fruit, “(..)(.)\1”, match = TRUE) (..) any 2 characters (.) any character 0 or more times. \1 repeating our first position, which is the (..)

So, for example (.)(.)(..)\2\1 1. (.) any character 2. (.) any character again 3. (..) two character \2 repeating THAT CHARACTER FROM POSITION 2 \1 repeating THAT CHARACTER FROM POSITION 1

Tips with backreference:

  • You must use () around the the thing you want to reference.
  • To backreference multiple times, use \\1 again.
  • The number refers to which spot you are referencing… e.g. \\2 references the second set of ()
x1 <- c("abxyba", "abccba", "xyaayx", "abxyab", "abcabc")
str_view(x1, "(.)(.)(..)\\2\\1")
str_view(x1, "(.)(.)(..)\\1\\2")
str_view(x1, "(.)(.)(.)\\1\\2\\3")
  1. Describe to your groupmates what these expressions will match, and provide a word or expression as an example:
  • "(.)\\1\\1"
  • "(.)(.)(.).*\\3\\2\\1"

For number 1, the expression will match one distinct character and repeat it twice (because we have two “\1”). Example: ooo

For number 2, it will match a combination of three characters (as we have 3 “(.)”, then the “.*” is saying that there will be any character 0 or more times then we are repeating that character from position 3, position 2, and position 1. Example: abchellocba

Which words in stringr::words match each expression?

str_view(stringr::words, "(.)\\1\\1")
str_view(stringr::words, "(.)(.)(.).*\\3\\2\\1")

For the first one, none. For the second one, “paragraph”.

  1. Construct a regular expression to match words in stringr::words that contain a repeated pair of letters (e.g. “church” contains “ch” repeated twice) but not match repeated pairs of numbers (e.g. 507-786-3861).
str_subset(stringr::words, "([a-z]{2}).*\\1")

  mutate(album_release_date = str_replace(album_release_date,
      "(\\d{4})-(\\d{2})-(\\d{2})",
      "\\2-\\3-\\1"))

  1. BEFORE RUNNING IT, explain to your partner(s) what the following R chunk will do:
sentences |> 
  str_replace("([^ ]+) ([^ ]+) ([^ ]+)", "\\1 \\3 \\2") |> 
  head(5)
[1] "The canoe birch slid on the smooth planks." 
[2] "Glue sheet the to the dark blue background."
[3] "It's to easy tell the depth of a well."     
[4] "These a days chicken leg is a rare dish."   
[5] "Rice often is served in round bowls."

Will show the first five row. ([^ ]+) basically capturing word (or anything that is not a space). Then in the replacement argument, we swap the position of word 3 and 2.
